Isaiah 1:1

The vision that Isaiah son of Amos saw, which he saw against Judea and against Jerusalem during the reign of Oziah, Joatham, Achaz and Hezekiah, who reigned over Judea.

  1. Hosea 1:1 parallel prophetic dating under the same Judean kings

    Isaiah and Hosea both date their prophetic revelation under Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz and Hezekiah, using the respective supplied name forms. Hosea additionally names Israel's Jeroboam.

    Read matching passage

    Hosea 1:1 The word of the Lord that came to Hosea son of Beeri in the days of Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz and Hezekiah, kings of Judah, and in the days of Jeroboam son of Joash, king of Israel.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 1:2

Listen, heaven, and give ear, earth, because the Lord has spoken: “I begot sons and raised them high, but they rejected Me.

  1. Deuteronomy 32:1-6 close heavenly-earthly summons and unfaithful children

    Both summon heaven and earth to hear before describing Israel as children who fail their divine Father. Isaiah speaks of sons raised high who reject Him; Deuteronomy calls the generation blemished and asks how it repays the Father who made it.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 32:1 Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak; and let the earth hear the words of my mouth.

    Deuteronomy 32:2 Let my utterance be awaited as the rain, and let my words descend as the dew, as a shower upon the grass, and as snow upon the herbage.

    Deuteronomy 32:3 For I have called upon the name of the Lord; ascribe greatness unto our God.

    Deuteronomy 32:4 God—true are His works, and all His ways are judgments; a faithful God, and there is no unrighteousness; righteous and holy is the Lord.

    Deuteronomy 32:5 Blemished children, not His, have sinned—a crooked and perverse generation.

    Deuteronomy 32:6 Do you thus repay the Lord, O foolish people and unwise? Is not He Himself your Father, who acquired you, and made you, and created you?

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 1:3

An ox knew the one who acquired it, and a donkey its lord’s manger; but Israel did not know Me, and the people did not understand Me.”

  1. Jeremiah 8:7 close animals knowing what God's people do not

    Isaiah contrasts an ox knowing its owner and a donkey its master's manger with Israel's failure to know God; Jeremiah contrasts birds observing their appointed times with the people's ignorance of the Lord's judgments. The animals and the knowledge specified differ.

    Read matching passage

    Jeremiah 8:7 Even the stork in heaven knew her time; turtledove and swallow, field sparrows, kept the times of their coming, but My people did not know the Lord’s judgments.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 1:4

Woe, sinful nation, people full of sins, evil offspring, lawless sons! You abandoned the Lord and provoked the Holy One of Israel to anger.

  1. Deuteronomy 32:15-20 close abandonment of the divine Father and provocation

    Isaiah indicts lawless sons for abandoning the Lord and provoking Him; Deuteronomy likewise describes Israel forsaking the God who begot and nourished it and provoking Him through unfaithfulness.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 32:15 And Jacob ate and was filled, and the beloved one kicked; he grew fat, he grew thick, he grew broad; and he forsook God who made him, and departed from God his Saviour.

    Deuteronomy 32:16 They provoked Me with foreign gods; with their abominations they embittered Me.

    Deuteronomy 32:17 They sacrificed unto demons, and not unto God, unto gods whom they did not know; new ones, lately come, whom their fathers did not know.

    Deuteronomy 32:18 You forsook God who begat you, and forgot God who nourishes you.

    Deuteronomy 32:19 And the Lord saw, and was jealous, and was provoked by the anger of His sons and daughters.

    Deuteronomy 32:20 And He said, I will turn My face from them, and show what shall befall them at the last; for they are a perverse generation, sons in whom is no faithfulness.

Isaiah 1:5

Why should you be struck again as you add lawlessness? Every head is in pain, and every heart in grief.

  1. Jeremiah 5:3 parallel punishment failing to produce correction

    Isaiah asks why further blows should fall as lawlessness increases; Jeremiah says the scourged people refuse instruction and harden themselves instead of returning. Neither presents the blows as having secured repentance.

    Read matching passage

    Jeremiah 5:3 Lord, Thine eyes are toward faithfulness. Thou didst scourge them, and they did not suffer; Thou broughtest them to an end, and they would not accept instruction. They hardened their faces beyond rock and would not return.

Isaiah 1:6

From feet to head: for neither a wound nor a bruise nor an inflamed blow is there a poultice to apply, nor oil nor bandages.

  1. Jeremiah 6:13-14 related untreated injury as a figure for the people

    Isaiah describes wounds without poultice, oil or bandages; Jeremiah likewise depicts the people's fracture being treated with contempt by those announcing peace. The latter specifies misleading religious leaders rather than Isaiah's catalogue of untreated injuries.

    Read matching passage

    Jeremiah 6:13 For from their small to their great, all have carried out lawless deeds; from priest to false prophet, all have done false things.

    Jeremiah 6:14 And they treated My people’s fracture with contempt, saying, ‘Peace, peace’; and where is peace?

Isaiah 1:7

Your land is deserted, your cities burned by fire. Your country—foreigners consume it before you; it has been deserted, overturned by foreign peoples.

  1. Leviticus 26:31-33 covenant-judgment counterpart to devastated land and cities

    Isaiah describes deserted land, burned cities and foreign consumption; Leviticus threatens desolated cities and land, enemies dwelling there and scattering among nations. Burning and consumption are Isaiah's particular details.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 26:31 and I will make your cities desolate, and lay waste your holy places; and I will in no wise smell the savour of your sacrifices;

    Leviticus 26:32 and I Myself will desolate your land, and your enemies who dwell in it shall marvel at it.

    Leviticus 26:33 And I will scatter you among the nations, and the advancing sword shall consume you. Your land shall become desolate, and your cities shall be desolate.

Isaiah 1:8

Daughter Zion will be left like a tent in a vineyard, like a fruit-watcher’s shelter in a cucumber field, like a besieged city.

  1. Isaiah 24:20 internal repeated fruit-watcher shelter image

    Isaiah 1 compares isolated Daughter Zion to a fruit-watcher's shelter; Isaiah 24 compares the shaken earth to the same kind of shelter. The fragile rural structure depicts distinct scales of devastation.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 24:20 The earth has tilted and will be shaken like a fruit-watcher’s shelter, like someone drunk and hungover. It will fall and will never be able to rise, for lawlessness has prevailed upon it.

    Romans expressly introduces Isaiah's words and repeats that without the Lord Sabaoth leaving seed, the people would have become like Sodom and Gomorrah.

Isaiah 1:10

Hear the Lord’s word, rulers of Sodom! Attend to God’s law, people of Gomorrah!

  1. Ezekiel 16:46-50 parallel prophetic comparison of Jerusalem with Sodom

    Isaiah addresses Jerusalem's rulers and people under Sodom and Gomorrah's names; Ezekiel calls Sodom Jerusalem's sister and says Jerusalem surpassed her lawlessness. These are moral comparisons, not a claim that the cities are geographically identical.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 16:46 Your elder sister is Samaria, she and her daughters, dwelling at your left; your sister younger than you, dwelling at your right, is Sodom and her daughters.

    Ezekiel 16:47 Not even so did you walk in their ways or act according to their lawlessness. Before long, you surpass them in all your ways.

    Ezekiel 16:48 As I live, says the Lord, your sister Sodom, she and her daughters, certainly did not do as you and your daughters have done.

    Ezekiel 16:49 Yet this was the lawlessness of your sister Sodom: arrogance. In fullness of bread and abundance of wine she and her daughters lived luxuriously. This belonged to her and her daughters, but they did not support the hand of the poor and needy.

    Ezekiel 16:50 They boasted greatly and committed acts of lawlessness before Me, and I removed them as I saw.

Isaiah 1:11

“What is the multitude of your sacrifices to Me?” says the Lord. “I am full of burnt offerings of rams; fat of lambs and blood of bulls and male goats I do not desire,

  1. Psalms 49:8-13 close divine rejection of reliance on sacrificial animals

    Isaiah says the Lord does not desire the abundance of rams, bulls' blood and goats; the psalm likewise refuses calves and goats and asks whether God eats bulls' flesh or drinks goats' blood. Their respective arguments challenge dependence on the offerings themselves.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 49:8 I will not rebuke you concerning your sacrifices; your burnt offerings are continually before Me.

    Psalms 49:9 I will not receive calves from your house or young goats from your flocks.

    Psalms 49:10 For all the forest’s wild animals are Mine, beasts on the mountains and cattle.

    Psalms 49:11 I know all the birds of heaven, and the field’s beauty is with Me.

    Psalms 49:12 If I become hungry, I will certainly not tell you, for the inhabited world and its fullness are Mine.

    Psalms 49:13 Will I eat bulls’ flesh or drink goats’ blood?

Isaiah 1:12

not even if you come to appear before Me. For who demanded these things from your hands? To trample My court,

  1. Micah 6:6-8 parallel questioning of what worship the Lord seeks

    Isaiah asks who demanded the worshipers' coming and trampling of His court; Micah asks whether offerings secure approach to God and answers with justice, mercy and readiness to walk with Him. Isaiah's surrounding summons to ethical reform supplies the comparable setting.

    Micah 6:7 Will the Lord receive with thousands of rams or with ten thousands of fat-rich torrents? Shall I give my firstborn offspring for impieties, the fruit of my body for the sin of my life?

    Micah 6:8 Has it been declared to you, O man, what is good? Or what does the Lord seek from you, except to do justice, and to love mercy, and to be ready to walk with the Lord your God?

you will no longer continue. If you bring fine flour, it is futile. Incense is an abomination to Me. Your new moons and sabbaths and a great day I cannot endure; fasting and idleness,

Isaiah 1:15

Whenever you stretch your hands toward Me, I will turn My eyes away from you. Even if you multiply supplication, I will not listen to you, for your hands are full of blood.

  1. Isaiah 59:1-3 internal bloodstained hands and withdrawn divine attention

    Isaiah 1 joins unanswered prayer and God's turned-away eyes with hands full of blood; Isaiah 59 likewise joins God's turned-away face with offenses and hands stained by blood. The latter explains that the barrier is sin, not inability to hear or save.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 59:1 Is the Lord’s hand unable to save? Or has His ear grown heavy so that He cannot hear?

    Isaiah 59:2 Instead, your offenses stand between you and God, and because of your sins He has turned His face away from you so as not to show mercy.

    Isaiah 59:3 For your hands are stained with blood and your fingers with sins. Your lips spoke lawlessness, and your tongue meditates on injustice.

    Isaiah promises that deeply colored sins will be whitened like snow and wool; the psalmist likewise expects divine washing to make him whiter than snow. Wool and the purple-red and scarlet colors belong to Isaiah.

Isaiah 1:21

How the faithful city Zion has become a prostitute, full of judgment, in which righteousness lay at rest, but now murderers!

  1. Ezekiel 16:8-15 parallel Jerusalem portrayed as an unfaithful woman

    Isaiah contrasts formerly faithful and righteous Zion with a prostitute and murderers; Ezekiel likewise describes Jerusalem brought into God's covenant and then prostituting herself through trust in her beauty. The accounts develop different details of the city's betrayal.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 16:9 I washed you with water, washed your blood off you, and anointed you with oil,

    Ezekiel 16:11 and adorned you with ornaments, placed bracelets around your hands and a necklace around your neck,

    Ezekiel 16:12 and put a ring around your nostril, little hoops upon your ears and a crown of boasting upon your head.

    Ezekiel 16:14 Your fame went out among the nations because of your beauty, for it was made complete in splendor, in the loveliness I placed upon you, says the Lord.

    Ezekiel 16:15 You trusted in your beauty and prostituted yourself because of your fame. You poured out your prostitution upon every passerby—which will not be.

  1. Jeremiah 6:28-30 close rejected silver as a figure for corruption

    Isaiah calls the city's silver unapproved; Jeremiah calls the corrupt people rejected silver because wickedness was not melted away and the Lord rejected them. Isaiah's diluted wine is a separate accompanying image.

    Jeremiah 6:30 Call them rejected silver, because the Lord rejected them.

Isaiah 1:23

Your rulers disobey, partners of thieves, loving gifts and pursuing repayment, not judging for orphans and not attending to widows’ judgment.

  1. Deuteronomy 10:17-18 contrast between corrupt rulers and God's impartial justice

    Isaiah's rulers love gifts and neglect orphans and widows; Deuteronomy describes God as taking no bribe and executing judgment for the orphan and widow. The rulers contradict the particular judicial qualities attributed to God.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 10:17 For the Lord your God, He is God of gods and Lord of lords, the great and mighty and fearful God, who shows no partiality, neither takes a bribe;

    Deuteronomy 10:18 who executes judgment for the sojourner, and the orphan, and the widow, and loves the sojourner, to give him bread and clothing.

Their strength will become a stalk of tow and their works sparks of fire. The lawless and sinners will be burned together, with no one to quench.”

    Isaiah makes strength a stalk of tow and works sparks of fire and says lawless people burn with no quencher; John the Baptist likewise uses rejected trees and chaff for judgment and names unquenchable fire. The images concern different forms of rejected growth and do not identify Isaiah's historical scene with the baptismal address.

    Read matching passage

  1. Ezekiel 36:25-27 parallel divine cleansing expressed with spirit language

    Isaiah promises removal of Zion's filth and blood by a spirit of judgment and burning; Ezekiel likewise promises cleansing from uncleanness and idols and God's Spirit within the people. Sprinkled water and inward renewal are Ezekiel's particular formulation.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 36:25 I will sprinkle clean water upon you, and you will be cleansed from all your uncleannesses and all your idols. I will cleanse you.

    Ezekiel 36:26 And I will give you a new heart, and I will give a new spirit within you; and I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh, and give you a heart of flesh.

    Ezekiel 36:27 I will put My Spirit within you and cause you to walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.

    Pinned source record

  2. Malachi 3:2-3 related purification through divine fiery judgment

    Isaiah's cleansing of Zion comes by a spirit of judgment and burning; Malachi describes the Lord's purifying arrival as smelting fire cleansing Levi's sons. This addresses the fiery-purification facet separately from Ezekiel's water and Spirit promise.

    Read matching passage

    Malachi 3:2 And who will endure the day of his entrance? Or who will stand at his appearance? For he enters like a smelting fire and like launderers’ herb.

    Malachi 3:3 He will sit, smelting and purifying as with silver and gold. He will cleanse the sons of Levi and melt them like gold and silver, and they will be those who bring a sacrifice to the Lord in righteousness.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 4:5

He will come, and it will be: a cloud will shade every place of Mount Zion and everything around her by day, and as of smoke and as of a light of fire burning by night; with all the glory it will be covered.

  1. Exodus 13:21-22 close cloud by day and fire by night

    Isaiah promises cloud-covering by day and fiery light by night over Zion; Exodus likewise describes God's persistent pillar of cloud by day and fire by night before Israel. The promised Zion covering and the wilderness guidance are distinct settings.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 13:21 God led them by day in a pillar of cloud to show them the way, and by night in a pillar of fire.

    Exodus 13:22 The pillar of cloud by day and the pillar of fire by night did not cease before all the people.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 4:6

It will be shade from heat, shelter and concealment from harshness and rain.

  1. Psalms 120:5-6 related divine shelter from scorching heat

    Isaiah promises shade from heat as part of God's shelter; the psalm likewise names the Lord as shelter and says the daytime sun will not scorch. Isaiah additionally includes harshness and rain.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 120:5 The Lord will guard you; the Lord is your shelter at your right hand.

    Psalms 120:6 By day the sun will not scorch you, nor the moon by night.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:1

I will sing now to the beloved a song of the loved one for my vineyard. The beloved had a vineyard on a horn, in a fertile place.

  1. Psalms 79:9-12 parallel cultivation of Israel as a vine

    Isaiah introduces the beloved's vineyard, identified as Israel in 5:7; the psalm portrays Israel as God's vine transplanted from Egypt and carefully rooted until its branches spread across the land.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 79:9 Thou didst transplant a vine from Egypt; Thou didst cast out nations and plant her.

    Psalms 79:10 Thou madest a road before her and didst plant her roots, and the land was filled.

    Psalms 79:11 Her shadow covered mountains, and her climbing branches covered God’s cedars.

    Psalms 79:12 She stretched her branches as far as the sea, and her offshoots as far as the River.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:2

I put a fence around it, surrounded it with a palisade, planted a Sorech vine, built a tower in its midst and dug a wine vat in it. I waited for it to produce grapes, but it produced thorns.

  1. Matthew 21:33 shared vineyard fence, tower, and winepress imagery

    Isaiah's carefully planted vineyard has a fence, tower, and wine vat; Jesus' vineyard parable repeats that distinctive arrangement. Isaiah's immediate failure is the vine's thorns, whereas the parable develops the tenants' wrongdoing.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 21:33 Hear another parable. A householder planted a vineyard, put a fence around it, dug a winepress in it, and built a tower. He leased it to farmers and traveled abroad.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:3

Now, man of Judah and residents of Jerusalem, judge My case and the matter of My vineyard.

  1. Micah 6:1-2 parallel presentation of God's case against His people

    Isaiah calls Judah and Jerusalem to judge the case concerning His vineyard; Micah explicitly announces the Lord's case against Israel before the hills and foundations of the earth. The judicial setting corresponds while the summoned audience differs.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 6:1 Hear now the word of the Lord. The Lord said: “Rise, enter into judgment before the mountains, and let the hills hear your voice.”

    Micah 6:2 Hear, hills, the Lord’s judgment, and you ravines, foundations of the earth! For the Lord has a case against His people, and He will argue it with Israel.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:4

What more shall I do for My vineyard that I did not do for it? For I waited for it to produce grapes, but it produced thorns.

  1. Micah 6:3-4 parallel divine questions after benefits bestowed

    The vineyard owner asks what more he could have done after expecting grapes; the Lord asks Israel how He has wronged them and recalls their redemption from Egypt. Both questions expose an unjustified response to prior divine care.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 6:3 “My people, what have I done to you? How have I grieved you, or how have I troubled you? Answer Me!

    Micah 6:4 For I brought you up from Egypt’s land and redeemed you from the house of slavery. I sent Moses, Aaron and Miriam before you.

    Pinned source record

  2. Hebrews 6:7-8 parallel failed crop despite provision

    Isaiah contrasts expected grapes with thorns after careful cultivation; Hebrews contrasts useful vegetation from rain-fed ground with thorns and thistles facing rejection. The shared agricultural argument concerns the response to received provision.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 6:7 For land that drinks the rain often coming upon it, and produces vegetation useful to those for whose sake it is cultivated, shares in blessing from God.

    Hebrews 6:8 But if it bears thorns and thistles, it is rejected and near a curse; its end is burning.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:5

Now I will announce to you what I will do to My vineyard: I will remove its fence, and it will be plundered; I will pull down its wall, and it will be trampled,

  1. Psalms 79:13-14 parallel removal of a vine's protective boundary

    Isaiah announces that removal of the vineyard's fence will expose it to plunder and trampling; the psalm asks why God tore down the vine's fence, allowing passersby and wild creatures to ravage it.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 79:13 Why didst Thou tear down her fence, so that all who pass along the road harvest her?

    Psalms 79:14 A pig from the woodland ravaged her, and a solitary wild beast grazed her down.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:6

and I will abandon My vineyard. It will certainly not be pruned or dug, and thorns will rise in it as in fallow ground. I will command the clouds not to rain upon it.

  1. Deuteronomy 28:23-24 parallel judgment through loss of nourishing rain

    God commands the clouds not to rain upon the abandoned vineyard; Deuteronomy's covenant warning replaces the land's rain with dust and earth. The target supports the withheld-rain judgment, not every detail of pruning and thorns.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 28:23 And the heaven above your head shall be bronze, and the earth beneath you iron.

    Deuteronomy 28:24 The Lord give dust for the rain of your land; and earth shall descend upon you from heaven, until He wears you away, and until He destroys you.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:7

For the vineyard of the Lord Sabaoth is Israel’s house, and the man of Judah a beloved new planting. I waited for it to produce judgment, but it produced lawlessness, not righteousness but an outcry.

  1. Jeremiah 2:21 parallel Israel as a good planting turned bad

    Isaiah identifies the vineyard as Israel and contrasts expected justice with lawlessness; Jeremiah addresses Israel as a fruitful, genuine vine that has changed into bitterness. Both use failed vine cultivation to expose the people's corruption.

    Read matching passage

    Jeremiah 2:21 But I planted you as a fruitful vine, wholly genuine. How have you changed into bitterness, you foreign vine?

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 5:8

Woe to those joining house to house and bringing field beside field, so that they may take something from their neighbor! Surely you will not dwell alone upon the earth?

  1. Micah 2:1-2 parallel seizure of neighbors' houses and fields

    Isaiah condemns joining houses and fields so as to take from a neighbor; Micah describes people coveting fields and plundering a man, his house, and his inheritance. Both connect expanding property with dispossession.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 2:1 They became people planning troubles and working evil on their beds, and at daybreak they carried them out, because they did not raise their hands to God.

    Micah 2:2 They coveted fields and plundered orphans, oppressed houses and plundered a man and his house, a man and his inheritance.

Isaiah 5:9

For these things were heard in the ears of the Lord Sabaoth: “If many houses come to be, great and beautiful, they will become deserted, and no one will inhabit them.

  1. Amos 3:14-15 related judgment upon costly houses

    Isaiah's great and beautiful houses become deserted; Amos announces judgment in which colonnaded and summer houses are struck and ivory houses perish. Both portray impressive housing as unable to escape judgment, with different stated outcomes.

    Read matching passage

    Amos 3:14 “because on the day I exact judgment upon Israel for its impieties, I will also exact judgment upon Bethel’s altars. The altar’s horns will be demolished and fall upon the ground,

    Amos 3:15 and I will confound and strike the colonnaded house along with the summer house. Ivory houses will perish, and many other houses will be added,” says the Lord.

Isaiah 5:10

For where ten pairs of oxen work, it will yield one jar, and the one sowing six artabas will yield three measures.”

  1. Deuteronomy 28:38-39 parallel disappointing grain and vineyard yields

    Isaiah gives sharply reduced yields from cultivated land and sown grain; Deuteronomy warns that much seed will yield little and cultivated vines will supply no wine. The correspondence concerns failed harvests, without equating the different measurements or causes.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 28:38 You shall carry much seed into the field, and gather little; for the locust shall devour it.

    Deuteronomy 28:39 You shall plant a vineyard and dress it, but shall neither drink wine nor rejoice in it; for the worm shall devour it.

Isaiah 5:11

Woe to those rising in the morning and pursuing strong drink, lingering into the evening, for wine will burn them up!

  1. Proverbs 23:29-35 parallel warning against prolonged pursuit of wine

    Isaiah condemns drinkers who pursue strong drink from morning and linger until evening; Proverbs identifies those lingering over wines and seeking drinking parties, then describes their injury and renewed craving.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 23:29 Who has woe? Who has uproar? Who has a lawsuit? Who has unpleasantness and chatter? Who has bruises for nothing? Whose eyes are livid?

    Proverbs 23:30 Is it not those who linger over wines? Is it not those who track down where drinking parties take place?

    Proverbs 23:31 Do not become drunk with wine, but associate with righteous people, and converse during walks; for if you give your eyes to bowls and cups, afterward you will walk about barer than a pestle.

    Proverbs 23:32 But at the end he lies stretched out as though struck by a snake, and as though by a horned viper its poison spreads through him.

    Proverbs 23:33 When your eyes see another woman, then your mouth will speak crooked things,

    Proverbs 23:34 and you will lie down as in the heart of the sea, and like a helmsman in heavy surging;

    Proverbs 23:35 and you will say, “They strike me, and I felt no pain; they mocked me, but I did not know. When will dawn come, so that I may go and seek those with whom I will gather?”

Isaiah 5:12

For with lyre and harp, drums and flutes they drink wine, but they do not look at the Lord’s works or consider the works of His hands.

  1. Amos 6:4-6 parallel music and wine amid culpable indifference

    Isaiah's musical wine feast disregards the Lord's works; Amos describes luxurious eating, instruments, and wine without suffering over Joseph's shattering. Both connect festive indulgence with neglected obligations, while the neglected object differs.

    Read matching passage

    Amos 6:4 sleeping upon ivory beds and indulging upon your couches, eating young goats from the flocks and milk-fed calves from the herds,

    Amos 6:5 clapping to the sound of instruments; they reckoned them as lasting, not passing away,

    Amos 6:6 drinking strained wine and anointing yourselves with the finest perfumes, yet suffering nothing over Joseph’s shattering.

Isaiah 5:13

Therefore My people became captive because they did not know the Lord, and there was a multitude of dead through famine and thirst for water.

  1. Hosea 4:6 parallel judgment associated with failure to know God

    Isaiah explains captivity and deadly deprivation by the people's not knowing the Lord; Hosea connects the people's lack of knowledge and rejection of recognition with divine rejection. Hosea does not repeat Isaiah's particular famine and captivity details.

    Read matching passage

    Hosea 4:6 My people were likened to one without knowledge. Because you thrust away recognition, I too will thrust you away from serving Me as priest. You forgot your God’s law, and I too will forget your children.

Isaiah 5:14

Hades enlarged its appetite and opened its mouth without ceasing, and her glorious ones, great ones, rich ones and pestilent ones will go down.

  1. Habakkuk 2:5 shared image of Hades' enlarged appetite

    Isaiah portrays Hades opening an unceasing mouth to receive the city's great and rich; Habakkuk compares a boastful man's widened, unsatisfied appetite to Hades and death. The image corresponds, while Isaiah describes Hades itself and Habakkuk a human resemblance.

    Read matching passage

    Habakkuk 2:5 But the wine-soaked, contemptuous, boastful man will accomplish nothing. He widened his appetite like Hades and is like death, never filled; he will gather all nations and receive all peoples to himself.

Isaiah 5:16

The Lord Sabaoth will be exalted through judgment, and the holy God will be glorified through righteousness.

  1. Ezekiel 28:22 parallel divine glory displayed through judgment

    Isaiah says the holy God will be exalted through judgment and glorified through righteousness; Ezekiel says the Lord will be glorified and sanctified in Sidon when He executes judgments there. The settings differ, but both explicitly join judgment and God's manifested honor.

    Read matching passage

Isaiah 5:17

Those who were plundered will be pastured like bulls, and lambs will feed upon the deserted places of those wiped out.

  1. Isaiah 7:25 related pasture imagery after the land's upheaval

    Isaiah 5 portrays plundered people pastured like bulls and lambs feeding in deserted places; Isaiah 7 describes land becoming sheep pasture and cattle trampling ground. Both use cattle and sheep in transformed land, without equating the figurative people of 5:17 with the animals of 7:25.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 7:25 Every mountain that is plowed will be plowed, and fear will certainly not come there; for from fallow ground and thorn it will become pasture for sheep and a trampling place for cattle.

Isaiah 5:18

Woe to those drawing sins as with a long cord and lawless deeds as with the strap of a heifer’s yoke,

  1. Proverbs 5:22 shared cords-of-sin imagery with differing agency

    Isaiah condemns people drawing sins with a long cord; Proverbs depicts people bound tightly by the cords of their own sins. The same binding image exposes attachment to wrongdoing, but Isaiah emphasizes drawing sins and Proverbs being caught by them.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 5:22 Lawless acts hunt a man, and each is bound tight by the cords of his own sins.

Isaiah 5:19

who say, “Let what He will do approach quickly, so that we may see, and let the counsel of the Holy One of Israel come, so that we may know!”

  1. Jeremiah 17:15 parallel taunt demanding arrival of the Lord's word

    Isaiah quotes those insisting that God's work and counsel come quickly so they may see; Jeremiah reports opponents asking where the Lord's word is and demanding that it come. Both challenge prophetic warning by demanding its realization.

    Read matching passage

Isaiah 5:20

Woe to those calling evil good and good evil, making darkness light and light darkness, making bitter sweet and sweet bitter!

  1. Proverbs 17:15 particular judicial instance of reversing good and evil

    Isaiah condemns calling evil good and good evil; Proverbs calls judging the unjust righteous and the righteous unjust detestable before God. The target supplies a concrete moral-verdict reversal, rather than repeating Isaiah's light and flavor images.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 17:15 One who judges the unjust righteous and the righteous unjust is unclean and detestable before God.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 3:7 Do not be prudent in your own estimation; fear God and turn away from every evil.

Isaiah 5:22

    Isaiah condemns strong and powerful people drinking and mixing intoxicants; Proverbs warns the powerful against wine lest they forget wisdom and become unable to judge the weak rightly. Isaiah's following verse similarly exposes corrupted judgment.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 31:5 so that, after drinking, they may not forget wisdom and become entirely unable to judge the weak rightly.

  1. Malachi 3:19 parallel fire judgment expressed through root and plant destruction

    Isaiah compares the condemned to a stalk burned by fire, with root like chaff and blossom like dust; Malachi depicts practitioners of lawlessness as straw burned until neither root nor branch remains. Both join fiery plant imagery to judgment on wrongdoing.

Isaiah 5:25

  1. Isaiah 9:11 repeated refrain of unspent anger and the high hand

    Both passages conclude that despite the announced blows the anger has not turned away and the hand is still high. This is a distinctive repeated judgment refrain, while the surrounding descriptions of the blows differ.

    Isaiah 9:11 Syria from the sun’s rising and the Greeks from the sun’s setting, who devour Israel with their whole mouth. For all these things the anger did not turn away, but the hand is still high.

  1. Amos 5:18-20 parallel darkness as the character of judgment

    Isaiah's threatened people look toward the earth and find harsh darkness and perplexity; Amos warns that the Lord's day is darkness rather than light. Both use darkness to describe the experience of judgment.

    Amos 5:20 Is not the Lord’s day darkness rather than light, gloom that has no brightness in it?

    Isaiah's seraphim have six wings, with pairs covering faces and feet and enabling flight; Ezekiel's living creatures have four wings, two of which cover their bodies. The comparison concerns winged attendants and covering, while preserving their different kinds, numbers, and arrangements.

    Isaiah's house fills with smoke at the attendants' cry; Sinai is covered with smoke at God's fiery descent, accompanied by strong trumpet sounds. Both associate the divine manifestation with smoke and overwhelming sound; Exodus here does not state that Isaiah's lintel event recurs.

    Exodus 19:18 Mount Sinai was entirely covered with smoke because God had descended on it in fire. The smoke rose like the smoke of a furnace, and all the people were greatly astonished.

  1. Luke 5:8-10 parallel confession of unworthiness before a manifestation of the Lord

Isaiah 6:6

And one of the seraphim was sent to me, with a coal in his hand that he had taken from the altar with the tongs;

  1. Leviticus 16:12-13 related altar coals brought into the divine presence

    Isaiah's seraph takes a coal from the altar for the encounter with the prophet; Aaron brings burning altar coals within the veil and uses incense before the Lord. The altar-coal setting corresponds, but the priestly incense rite is not the same action as Isaiah's cleansing.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 16:12 And he shall take the firepan full of burning coals from the altar opposite the Lord, fill his hands with finely compounded incense, and bring them within the veil,

    Leviticus 16:13 and put the incense on the fire before the Lord. The vapour of incense shall cover the propitiatory cover above the testimonies, and he shall not die.

and he touched my mouth and said, Behold, this has touched your lips, and will take away your iniquities and cleanse your sins.

  1. Jeremiah 1:9 parallel touching of a prophet's mouth before commission

    Isaiah's lips are touched with the coal to remove iniquity and cleanse sins; the Lord touches Jeremiah's mouth to put His words there. Both prepare a prophet's speech through a touch, while the explicitly stated effects differ.

    The Lord asks whom to send and Isaiah volunteers; in Micaiah's vision the Lord asks who will deceive Ahab and a spirit volunteers. The council question-and-response pattern corresponds; Isaiah's prophetic mission is not equated with the spirit's deceptive mission.

  1. Acts 28:25-27 explicit quotation of Isaiah's unresponsive people

    John uses Isaiah's eye-and-heart language to explain unbelief toward Jesus, stating that He blinded their eyes and hardened their heart. Isaiah's supplied wording says the people closed their eyes; the connection preserves this difference rather than substituting one form for the other.

Isaiah 8:14

If you trust in Him, He will become a sanctuary for you, and you will not encounter Him as the stumbling of a stone or as the falling of a rock. But Jacob’s house is in a trap, and those settled in Jerusalem in a pit.

  1. Romans 9:32-33 reuse of stumbling-stone language in a composite scriptural citation

    Isaiah contrasts trusting sanctuary with encountering a stumbling stone or falling rock; Romans uses the stone of stumbling and rock of offense to explain Israel's stumbling because its pursuit was as though from works, not from faith. The citation develops the stone image without erasing Isaiah's protection for those who trust.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 9:33 just as it is written, “Behold, I lay in Zion a stone of stumbling and a rock of offense, and the one who believes in Him will not be put to shame.”

    Pinned source record

  2. 1 Peter 2:6-8 Christological use of the stone and differing responses

    First Peter applies the stone-of-stumbling wording to disobedience toward Christ, while distinguishing the honor of believers. Isaiah likewise distinguishes those trusting the Lord from the trapped house, though its supplied wording presents sanctuary and avoidance of stumbling conditionally.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 2:7 Therefore, the honor is for you who believe; but for those who do not believe, “The stone that the builders rejected has become the head of the corner,”

    1 Peter 2:8 and “a stone of stumbling and a rock of offense.” They stumble, disobeying the word; to this they were also appointed.

    Isaiah places an altar and a border pillar for the Lord within Egypt, called a sign in the following verse. Joshua's border dispute is answered by an altar serving as a witness to shared belonging to the Lord. Joshua expressly excludes sacrificial use for that witness altar, so the comparison concerns public testimony rather than identical ritual rules.

  1. Jeremiah 31:44 parallel escape-and-capture sequence

    Both passages say that fleeing fear leads into a pit and climbing from the pit leads into a trap. The successive failed escapes are a close verbal and structural parallel.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah connects the failed escapes with heaven's opened windows and shaken earthly foundations. Genesis describes the opening of heaven's floodgates when the flood begins. Isaiah 27:1

On that day God will bring the holy, great and strong sword against the dragon, the fleeing serpent, against the dragon, the crooked serpent, and will kill the dragon.

  1. Psalms 73:13-14 divine victory over dragons

    Isaiah announces God's killing the dragon with His great strong sword. The psalm likewise celebrates God's crushing the heads of dragons upon the water and the dragon's heads. Both use the defeat of a formidable dragon to portray divine power; no further identification of the creature is required by the comparison.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 73:13 Thou didst strengthen the sea by Thy power; Thou didst crush the dragons’ heads upon the water.

    Psalms 73:14 Thou didst crush the dragon’s heads; Thou gavest him as food to the Ethiopian peoples.

    Pinned source record

  2. Job 26:13 divine killing of the dragon

    Isaiah's sword judgment targets the fleeing and crooked serpent described as a dragon. Job likewise credits God with killing the rebellious dragon by command. The shared decisive divine victory is explicit, although the dragon's descriptors and the means pictured differ.

    Read matching passage

    Job 26:13 The bars of heaven have feared Him, and by a command He killed the rebellious dragon.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 27:2

On that day, a beautiful vineyard; a desire to lead a song against her.

  1. Isaiah 5:1-7 literary vineyard-song parallel

    Isaiah 27 introduces a beautiful vineyard and a song against her. Isaiah 5 likewise opens a vineyard song, then turns its cultivation story into a charge against Israel's failed righteousness. Both use the vineyard-song form for a communal address with judgment in its context, rather than presuming an uncomplicated celebration of a guarded vineyard.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 5:1 I will sing now to the beloved a song of the loved one for my vineyard. The beloved had a vineyard on a horn, in a fertile place.

    Isaiah 5:2 I put a fence around it, surrounded it with a palisade, planted a Sorech vine, built a tower in its midst and dug a wine vat in it. I waited for it to produce grapes, but it produced thorns.

    Isaiah 5:3 Now, man of Judah and residents of Jerusalem, judge My case and the matter of My vineyard.

    Isaiah 5:4 What more shall I do for My vineyard that I did not do for it? For I waited for it to produce grapes, but it produced thorns.

    Isaiah 5:5 Now I will announce to you what I will do to My vineyard: I will remove its fence, and it will be plundered; I will pull down its wall, and it will be trampled,

    Isaiah 5:6 and I will abandon My vineyard. It will certainly not be pruned or dug, and thorns will rise in it as in fallow ground. I will command the clouds not to rain upon it.

    Isaiah 5:7 For the vineyard of the Lord Sabaoth is Israel’s house, and the man of Judah a beloved new planting. I waited for it to produce judgment, but it produced lawlessness, not righteousness but an outcry.

Isaiah 27:3

“I am a strong city, a besieged city; in vain I will water her, for she will be captured by night, and by day the wall will fall.

  1. Isaiah 5:5-6 literary failure of vineyard defenses

    Following the vineyard-song introduction, Isaiah 27 places a besieged city, watering in vain, night capture and a falling wall together. Isaiah 5 likewise removes the vineyard's fence and wall, leaving it trampled and without rain. Both depict failed protection in a vineyard oracle; the native watering in vain is not replaced by continual safe watering.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 5:5 Now I will announce to you what I will do to My vineyard: I will remove its fence, and it will be plundered; I will pull down its wall, and it will be trampled,

    Isaiah 5:6 and I will abandon My vineyard. It will certainly not be pruned or dug, and thorns will rise in it as in fallow ground. I will command the clouds not to rain upon it.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 27:5

the residents in her will cry. “Let us make peace with Him; let us make peace.”

  1. Isaiah 57:18-19 peace sought and granted after affliction

    Isaiah 27's residents cry to make peace with Him amid the preceding burning and rejection. Isaiah 57 answers an earlier account of divine discipline with healing, comfort and peace upon peace. Both place restored peace in relation to the Lord after affliction, one as the residents' plea and the other as divine assurance.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 57:18 I have seen his ways, healed him, comforted him, and given him true comfort—

    Isaiah 57:19 peace upon peace to those far away and those near. And the Lord said, ‘I will heal them.’

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 27:6

Those coming, Jacob’s children—Israel will sprout and flower, and the inhabited world will be filled with his fruit.

  1. Hosea 14:6-8 agricultural image of Israel's restoration

    Isaiah promises that Jacob's children and Israel will sprout, flower and fill the inhabited world with fruit. Hosea likewise promises Israel blossom, roots, spreading branches and the fruitfulness of olive and vine. Both describe the people's renewal through flourishing plants rather than through military expansion.

    Read matching passage

    Hosea 14:6 I will be like dew to Israel; he will blossom like a lily and put down his roots like Lebanon.

    Hosea 14:7 His branches will spread, and he will be like an olive tree laden with fruit; his fragrance will be like Lebanon.

    Hosea 14:8 They will return and settle beneath his shelter; they will live and be filled to intoxication with grain. His memorial will blossom like a vine, like Lebanon’s wine.

Isaiah 27:9

Therefore Jacob’s lawlessness will be removed, and this is his blessing: when I remove his sin, when they make all altar stones cut down like fine dust. Their trees will certainly not remain, and their idols will be cut down like a forest far away.

  1. Romans 11:26-27 verbal correspondence in removal of sin

    Isaiah links removal of Jacob's lawlessness with God's declaration about removing his sin. Romans likewise names the turning away of ungodliness from Jacob and concludes its written citation with God taking away their sins. The shared wording concerns Jacob's purification; Isaiah's word blessing and Romans' word covenant are preserved distinctly.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 11:26 and in this way all Israel will be saved, just as it is written: “The Deliverer will come from Zion; He will turn away ungodliness from Jacob.

    Romans 11:27 And this is My covenant with them, when I take away their sins.”

    Pinned source record

  2. 2 Kings 23:14-15 narrative illustration of destroyed cult objects

    Isaiah joins removal of Jacob's sin with altar stones reduced to fine dust and the cutting down of trees and idols. Kings recounts Josiah shattering pillars, destroying groves and reducing Bethel's altar stones to dust. The acts give a concrete narrative counterpart to the dismantling of illicit worship described by Isaiah.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 23:14 He shattered the pillars, destroyed the groves, and filled their sites with human bones.

    2 Kings 23:15 Moreover, he tore down the altar at Bethel, the high place Jeroboam son of Nabat had made, who caused Israel to sin—both that altar and the high place. He shattered its stones, reduced it to dust, and burned the grove.

    Pinned source record

Isaiah 27:10

The inhabited flock will be released like an abandoned flock; it will be pasture for a long time, and there they will rest.

  1. Isaiah 17:2 literary pasture-and-rest image

    Isaiah 27 describes a released, abandoned flock with prolonged pasture and rest. Isaiah 17 describes an abandoned city becoming a resting place for flocks. Both bring flock, abandonment and rest together within judgment oracles; the native flock wording in Isaiah 27 is not silently rewritten as a fortified city.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 17:2 abandoned for everlasting time, a bed and resting place for flocks, with no one pursuing.

Isaiah 27:11

After a time there will be nothing green in her because it has dried. Women coming from a spectacle, come! For this is a people without understanding; therefore their Maker will certainly not pity them, nor will the One who formed them ever have mercy.

  1. Isaiah 1:3-4 uncomprehending people under judgment

    Isaiah 27 explains the Maker's refusal of pity by the people's lack of understanding. Isaiah 1 likewise says Israel does not know and the people do not understand, then names their abandonment and provocation of the Lord. Both connect failure to recognize the One who formed or raised them with the ensuing indictment.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 1:3 An ox knew the one who acquired it, and a donkey its lord’s manger; but Israel did not know Me, and the people did not understand Me.”

    Isaiah 1:4 Woe, sinful nation, people full of sins, evil offspring, lawless sons! You abandoned the Lord and provoked the Holy One of Israel to anger.

Isaiah 27:12

On that day the Lord will fence off from the River’s canal to Rhinocorura; but you, gather Israel’s sons one by one.

  1. Deuteronomy 30:3-4 gathering dispersed Israel

    Isaiah marks the Lord's boundary from the River's canal to Rhinocorura and calls for Israel's sons to be gathered one by one. Deuteronomy promises God's gathering of Israel from even the remotest dispersion. Both envision the scattered people collected again; Isaiah's command and exact place name remain distinct from Deuteronomy's promise.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 30:3 then the Lord shall heal your sins, and have mercy upon you, and shall gather you again from all the nations whither the Lord has scattered you.

    Deuteronomy 30:4 If your dispersion is from one end of heaven unto the other end of heaven, from thence shall the Lord your God gather you, and from thence shall the Lord your God take you.

Isaiah 27:13

On that day they will sound the great trumpet. Those lost in the Assyrians’ country and those lost in Egypt will come and bow to the Lord upon the holy mountain in Jerusalem.

  1. Isaiah 11:11-12 gathering from Assyria and Egypt

    Isaiah 27 brings the lost from Assyria's country and Egypt to worship at Jerusalem. Isaiah 11 likewise names Assyria and Egypt among the places from which the Lord gathers His remaining people and the scattered of Israel. The repeated locations and gathering of the dispersed make the connection specific.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 11:11 On that day the Lord will again show His hand, to be zealous for the remaining remnant of the people left from the Assyrians, Egypt, Babylonia, Ethiopia, the Elamites, the sun’s rising and Arabia.

    Isaiah 11:12 He will raise a signal to the nations and gather Israel’s lost ones; He will gather Judah’s scattered ones from the earth’s four wings.

    Pinned source record

  2. Matthew 24:31 trumpet and gathering parallel

    Isaiah sounds the great trumpet as dispersed people come to worship the Lord at Jerusalem. Matthew likewise joins a great trumpet with the gathering of the chosen from the four winds. The shared trumpet-and-gathering pattern is explicit, while Matthew's angels and Isaiah's named countries retain their different settings.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 24:31 He will send His angels with a great trumpet, and they will gather His chosen from the four winds, from one end of the heavens to the other.
